<div class="display-inline-block"><button type="button" class="cursor-pointer text-no-underline bg-transparent border-0 padding-0 text-left margin-0 text-normal text-primary" aria-controls="journal_context_menu">Neuroinformatics</button></div>. 2025 Jan 23;23(2):17. doi: <a href="https://doi.org/10.1007/s12021-024-09698-y" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.1007/s12021-024-09698-y</a>

<hgroup><h1>Determination of the Time-frequency Features for Impulse Components in EEG Signals</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">
<a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Filimonova%20N"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id1"><span class="name western">Natalia Filimonova</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id1">
<h3><span class="name western">Natalia Filimonova</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>1</sup>Biology and Medicine Institute Science Educational Center, Taras Shevchenko National University of Kyiv, Volodymyrska St, 60, Kyiv, 01033 Ukraine </div>
<div class="p">

<sup>1,</sup><sup>2</sup>, <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Specovius-Neugebauer%20M"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id2"><span class="name western">Maria Specovius-Neugebauer</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id2">

<div class="p">

<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Accepted 2024 Nov 27; Issue date 2025.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">

<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC11757888  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39847149/" class="usa-link">39847149</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="Abs1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p id="Par1">Accurately identifying the timing and frequency characteristics of impulse components in EEG signals is essential but limited by the Heisenberg uncertainty principle. Inspired by the visual system’s ability to identify objects and their locations, we propose a new method that integrates a visual system model with wavelet analysis to calculate both time and frequency features of local impulses in EEG signals. We develop a mathematical model based on invariant pattern recognition by the visual system, combined with wavelet analysis using Krawtchouk functions as the mother wavelet. Our method precisely identifies the localization and frequency characteristics of the impulse components in EEG signals. Tested on task-related EEG data, it accurately detected blink components (0.5 to 1 Hz) and separated muscle artifacts (16 Hz). It also identified muscle response durations (298 ms) within the 1 to 31 Hz range in emotional reaction studies, offering insights into both individual and typical emotional responses. We further illustrated how the new method circumvents the uncertainty principle in low-frequency wavelet analysis. Unlike classical wavelet analysis, our method provides spectral characteristics of EEG impulses invariant to time shifts, improving the identification and classification of EEG components.</p>
<section id="sec1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Information</h3>
<p>The online version contains supplementary material available at 10.1007/s12021-024-09698-y.</p></section><section id="kwd-group1" lang="en" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Visual system, Wavelet analysis, Krawtchouk functions, EEG processing</p></section></section><section id="Sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p id="Par3">When analyzing EEG signals, it is crucial to have information about their impulse components, such as blinks, muscle artifacts, bursts of brain activity, etc. At the same time, information about the time of impulse appearance, i.e., its localization and frequency characteristics, which allow its identification, is essential for further analysis. For example, in brain-computer interface (BCI) systems, it is necessary to identify local reactions for their further analysis or removal. These motor and other events are associated with focal changes in the brain activity and spatio-temporal patterns of EEG (Guger et al., <a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">2000</a>). Also, determination of the localization of the impulse component is necessary in studying the dynamics of processes, not only their average characteristics. Unfortunately, Heisenberg’s principle for Fourier analysis states that it is impossible to determine both the exact location of an impulse and its frequency features. Applied to Fourier analysis, it means that the greater the concentration of a function in space, the more “smeared” and uncertain its Fourier transform should be Bonami et al. (<a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">2003</a>). The Fourier transform is a traditional method for identifying a signal’s spectral properties. Applying the Fourier transform is based on the assumption that the signal is stationary and ergodic (Trethewey, <a href="#CR61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR61">2003</a>). However, this allows estimating the signal characteristics only in average but not the dynamics. One approach for analyzing non-stationary signals is to use a windowed Fourier transform. While using this method a non-stationary signal is pre-divided into segments (windows) where the signal is considered as stationary. However, the Heisenberg uncertainty principle for Fourier analysis (Bonami et al., <a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">2003</a>) makes it impossible to determine time and frequency simultaneously. We can only state that the coordinate resolution of a windowed transform is determined by the width of the window function and is inversely proportional to the frequency resolution (Mallat, <a href="#CR44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR44">2009</a>). For the Fourier and the windowed Fourier transforms these restrictions are fundamental (Bonami et al., <a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">2003</a>). Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) for signal processing can be particularly problematic when the signal consists of randomly occurring transients superimposed on a more continuous signal (Trethewey, <a href="#CR61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR61">2003</a>). Unfortunately, for example, most of the signals resulting in the brain are non-stationary and non-ergodic. Applying the classical spectral analysis and the Fourier transformation in the window to study the dynamics of the signals negatively affects the results. If the task is to investigate the reaction in the brain, it is necessary to extract information about the time and the frequency of some events in the signal. In this context wavelet analysis turned up as a solution to the problem of pinpointing local events. Morle and Grossman (1982-1984) (Mallat, <a href="#CR44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR44">2009</a>) proposed to use wavelet transforms of nonstationary signals. Here the non-stationary signal is decomposed into certain basic functions (other than harmonic), which are obtained from a prototype function by compression and shift. The prototype function is called the mother (basic) wavelet (Mallat, <a href="#CR44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR44">2009</a>). Usually one of following functions is used as mother wavelet: Mexican Hat, Daubechies Wavelet, Haar Wavelet, Morlet Wavelet and others (Mallat, <a href="#CR44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR44">2009</a>; Walnut, <a href="#CR69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR69">2002</a>). Among the most effective wavelets we find the Hermitian wavelets - a family of continuous wavelets, which are defined as the derivative of a Gaussian distribution (Mehra et al., <a href="#CR46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR46">2018</a>). These wavelets are very suitable for the analysis of signals containing some peaks, for example for the detection of singularities generated by localized defects in a mechanical system (Li et al., <a href="#CR36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR36">2011</a>; Zhang et al., <a href="#CR77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR77">2018</a>; Deng et al., <a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">2017</a>; Li &amp; Zhang, <a href="#CR42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR42">2020</a>). But Hermitian functions are functions of a continuous argument which leads to difficulties in their application when implementing appropriate algorithms. In this context we propose to use Krawtchouk functions (Vainerman &amp; Filimonova, <a href="#CR66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR66">1994</a>; Vainerman, <a href="#CR65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR65">1992</a>) as mother wavelets. Krawtchouk functions are the discrete analogue of Hermitian functions by using a simple idea: since polynomials are completely determined by their values at a sufficiently large number of different points, it is possible to define orthogonality (and also orthonormality) relations on certain polynomials by using only a discrete finite set of points. Their application to automated spectral analysis of arbitrary signals is free from the drawbacks which appear while using polynomials as functions of a continuous argument. Thus, our proposal to use the discrete Krawchouk functions as wavelets in signal processing is an advantage over classical approaches, but does not yet solve all problems. Also, wavelets fit into the principle of uncertainty because a basic wavelet is defined on a short time interval which corresponds to high frequency. When the wavelet is stretched this time interval gets longer and the frequency decreases (Mallat, <a href="#CR44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR44">2009</a>; Vošvrda &amp; Schurrer, <a href="#CR68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR68">2015</a>; Bonami et al., <a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">2003</a>). This is also true for Hermite wavelets (Bonami et al., <a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">2003</a>; Ricaud &amp; Torrésani, <a href="#CR52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR52">2014</a>; Wigderson &amp; Wigderson, <a href="#CR70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR70">2021</a>).</p>
<p id="Par4">Thus, the Heisenberg uncertainty principle does not allow solving the problem of simultaneously determining the localization of the impulse component of the signal and its frequency characteristics either by classical Fourier analysis or wavelet analysis. Both of these methods have fundamental limitations when trying to give precise answers to the questions “What?” and “When?”. However, our visual system is perfectly capable of answering the questions “What?” and “Where?” which, for one-dimensional signals, is equivalent to the question “When?” (Graumann et al., <a href="#CR23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR23">2022</a>). Therefore, we propose the next approach. In the first stage, we propose building a mathematical model of pattern recognition invariance by a visual system. We plan to incorporate this model into wavelet analysis at the next stage. We hypothesize that integrating the model of extraction of shift-invariant signal features into wavelet analysis will allow us to solve the problem of simultaneously accurate computation of the time coordinate of a local impulse in EEG signal and its frequency features. This integration will enable us to bypass the limitations imposed by the Heisenberg uncertainty principle.</p></section><section id="Sec2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">The Model of Shift Invariant Image Recognition of the Visual System</h2>
<p id="Par5">The problem of extracting a complete system of invariant features of a signal arises in signal processing (including images) as well as in automatic pattern recognition or automatic classification and diagnostics. It is necessary to separate the information about the characteristics of the signal from the information about the transformations that this signal has undergone. These transformations (e.g. shift, image rotation, scale conversion, etc.) cannot be controlled, a visual system must identify the image independent from its location, and the transformations should not affect the performance of the system. Therefore, the images that pass into each other under some specific transformations must be classified as equivalent.</p>
<p id="Par6">It should be noted that the human or mammalian visual system to some extent is capable to extract the invariant features of a signal. It took some effort to understand the functions necessary for visual form perception. Based on the knowledge about the receptive field (RF) reactions and about the visual system in Glezer (<a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">1995</a>); Kulikowski and Bishop (<a href="#CR34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR34">1981</a>); Lindeberg (<a href="#CR39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR39">2013</a>); Shapley and Lennie (<a href="#CR57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR57">1985</a>); Schnitzler (<a href="#CR56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR56">1976</a>) a theory was developed according to which the visual system performs spatial-frequency image filtering. To adapt this effect to general signal processing we have to understand how this filtering works. Let us consider in more detail the visual path from the retina to the cerebral cortex of primates. Visual information enters the retina and is transmitted to the brain through about a million nerve fibers that are united in the optic nerve (Jeffries et al., <a href="#CR31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR31">2014</a>; Hubel &amp; Weisel, <a href="#CR26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR26">1977</a>). Most of the optic nerve fibers reach without interruption two cell nuclei that are located deep in the brain. These nuclei are called lateral geniculate nuclei (LGN). In turn, neurons of the LGN send their axons directly to the primary visual cortex (Jeffries et al., <a href="#CR31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR31">2014</a>; Ghodrati et al., <a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">2017</a>).</p>
<p id="Par7">The RF of a retinal ganglion cell refers to the synaptic network of photoreceptors, bipolar, horizontal, and amacrine cells which come together to this one ganglion cell (Hubel &amp; Weisel, <a href="#CR26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR26">1977</a>). A concentric RF has the central zone where the receptors are stimulated to give response and a peripheral inhibitory ring (off-on), or conversely, an inhibitory central zone and a peripheral ring that gives response (on-off). Concentric fields are used to describe the image by points (Hubel &amp; Weisel, <a href="#CR26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR26">1977</a>). Mathematically, the spatiotemporal RF is defined by an impulse response function (weight function) describing the firing-rate response to a tiny spot which is on for a very short time (Mobarhan et al., <a href="#CR47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR47">2018</a>). Some methods of modelling this weight functions were developed with difference of arouse and inhibitory Gaussians (Mobarhan et al., <a href="#CR47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR47">2018</a>; Eiber et al., <a href="#CR17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR17">2018</a>; Einevoll &amp; Heggelund, <a href="#CR18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR18">2000</a>; Lindeberg, <a href="#CR40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR40">2021</a>; Jacobsen et al., <a href="#CR29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR29">2016</a>; Bertalmío, <a href="#CR10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR10">2019</a>), with Gabor elements (Paik &amp; Ringach, <a href="#CR48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR48">2011</a>; Lee et al., <a href="#CR35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR35">2016</a>; Cope et al., <a href="#CR14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR14">2009</a>) and others (Li et al., <a href="#CR37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR37">year</a>; Bertalmío, <a href="#CR10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR10">2019</a>).</p>
<p id="Par8">Retinal ganglion cells project sensory information to the lateral geniculate neurons of the thalamus. LGN neurons replicate the center-surround structure of their presynaptic partners (Hubel &amp; Weisel, <a href="#CR27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR27">1962</a>). Yet, this does not mean that the thalamic fields are direct copies of those in the retina. A single LGN neuron might receive inputs from multiple ganglion cells, hence the spatial information sent from retina is remixed (Alonso et al., <a href="#CR2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR2">2006</a>). One LGN neuron is overlapped with the ON (OFF) sub-region of the RF of retinal cells according to feedforward or feedback excitation (Usrey et al., <a href="#CR64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR64">1999</a>; Lian et al., <a href="#CR38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR38">2019</a>). Hence RFs of the LGN describe spatial patterns of light and dark regions around an average illuminance level in the visual field (Hubel &amp; Weisel, <a href="#CR26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR26">1977</a>; Lian et al., <a href="#CR38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR38">2019</a>; Liu et al., <a href="#CR41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR41">2021</a>). The neural networks of the visual cortex do not present a contour picture of the incoming image which exists on the retina (Glezer, <a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">1995</a>). For effective processing of incoming signals it is therefore necessary to reduce the information redundancy, which, based on known studies, can be assumed to be realized in the LGN (Zabbah et al., <a href="#CR73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR73">2014</a>). Let us look into more details of this process.</p>
<section id="FPar1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 1</h3>
<p id="Par9">The first transformation of information in the visual system is already threefold: informations are transformed from photoreceptors through horizontal cells to bipolar cells (Barnes et al., <a href="#CR9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR9">2020</a>). The optical system of the eye projects the image on the retinal layer of photoreceptors, while a horizontal cell summarizes the arousal of a large number of photoreceptors. Then the average of the summarized signals is subtracted (using the reverse suppressed signal) from the signals coming from the photoreceptors to the bipolar cells (Yeonan-Kim &amp; Bertalmío, <a href="#CR72" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR72">2016</a>). This is because the horizontal cells relate the photoreceptors and bipolar cells with sufficient long connections which are parallel to the layers of the retina (Glezer, <a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">1995</a>; Hubel &amp; Weisel, <a href="#CR26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR26">1977</a>). The human eye can operate in a wide range of illumination changes: about 11 orders, whereas separate neurons in the retina and also cells on a more higher layer may change the activity within only 2 orders. This small change is one of the components of the adaptation process to the level of illumination (Zapp et al., <a href="#CR75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR75">2022</a>). After the adaptation a new zero level is created (Barnes et al., <a href="#CR9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR9">2020</a>). The distribution of the illumination can now be described in terms of areas which are brighter or darker relative to the average level (Glezer, <a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">1995</a>; Yeonan-Kim &amp; Bertalmío, <a href="#CR72" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR72">2016</a>; Barnes et al., <a href="#CR9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR9">2020</a>). Hence, in the first step we characterize the field of vision by <em>M</em> input signal points <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq1"><math id="d33e421"><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mtext>input</mtext></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq2"><math id="d33e440"><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></math></span>. We model the distribution function <em>y</em>(<em>i</em>) of illumination transmitted to the receptive fields of the retinal ganglion cells (the first transformation of the signal) by</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ11"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="d33e462" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mi>y</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mtext>input</mtext></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>-</mo><mover><msub><mi>y</mi><mtext>input</mtext></msub><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>,</mo><mspace width="4pt"></mspace><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>,</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq3"><math id="d33e510"><mrow><mover><msub><mi>y</mi><mtext>input</mtext></msub><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>=</mo><mo>∑</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mtext>input</mtext></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></math></span> is the average of the illumination for the visual field. Contacts of bipolars with ganglion cells are executed by amacrine cells that play the role of interneurons, and under transition from bipolars to ganglion cells the arousal transforms the analog impulse to a digital one, which justify the use of discrete functions to simulate the processes in the visual system. Applying this transformation to the incoming signal, we provide the invariance property under illumination variations (Lindeberg, <a href="#CR39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR39">2013</a>).</p></section><section id="FPar2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 2</h3>
<p id="Par10">The concentric RF responds with a certain number of impulses per unit time (Hubel &amp; Weisel, <a href="#CR26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR26">1977</a>) (with discrete functions). Consequently, an adequate simulation should use functions of discrete arguments. The LGN is increasingly regarded as a “smart-gating” operator for processing visual information in which the relatively elaborate features are computed early in the visual pathway (Tang et al., <a href="#CR60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR60">2016</a>). It was shown that at high contrasts neurons of LGN tuned to higher frequencies, which enhances the use of high frequency in the analysis of images (Glezer, <a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">1995</a>). As contrast increases, the RFs overlap and the response function becomes alternating with respect to the average illuminance level (Glezer, <a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">1995</a>). For efficient coding in digital image processing linear filters should be orthogonal (Cho &amp; Choi, <a href="#CR13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR13">2014</a>). Therefore, in Cho and Choi (<a href="#CR13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR13">2014</a>)it was proposed to describe the RF of a retinal ganglion cells in terms of an orthogonal wavelet basis. Since the structure of the LGN inherits the structure of the retina RF, we can assume the efficiency of using orthogonal basis functions for modeling the LGN RF.</p>
<p id="Par11">Using experimental data regarding the visual system (Glezer, <a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">1995</a>; Hubel &amp; Weisel, <a href="#CR26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR26">1977</a>) it was suggested to describe the weight functions of the RF of the LGN with Krawtchouk functions (Vainerman &amp; Filimonova, <a href="#CR66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR66">1994</a>; Vainerman, <a href="#CR65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR65">1992</a>). Polynomials which are orthogonal on the set of points <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq4"><math id="d33e578"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></math></span> with respect to the binomial distribution were introduced by Mykhailo Krawtchouk, hence the denomination Krawtchouk functions. They are also called normalized Krawtchouk polynomials (Vainerman, <a href="#CR65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR65">1992</a>; Al-Utaibi et al., <a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">2021</a>; Jahid et al., <a href="#CR30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR30">2017</a>; Karmouni et al., <a href="#CR32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR32">2017</a>) in analogy with Hermite functions referring the fact that Krawtchouk polynomials are the discrete analogues of Hermite polynomials (Krawtchouk, <a href="#CR33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR33">1929</a>; Area et al., <a href="#CR5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR5">2014</a>), or weighted Krawtchouk polynomials ( Venkataramana and Raj, <a href="#CR67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR67">2011</a>; Idan et al., <a href="#CR28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR28">2020</a>; Yap et al., <a href="#CR71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR71">2003</a>), weighted and normalized Krawtchouk polynomials (Zhang et al., <a href="#CR76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR76">2010</a>; Abdulhussain et al., <a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">2018</a>; Asli &amp; Flusser, <a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">2014</a>) or Krawtchouk functions (den Brinker, <a href="#CR15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR15">2021</a>).</p>
<p id="Par12">To give a precise description of these functions we recall the definition of the binomial distribution <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq5"><math id="d33e639"><mrow><mi>ρ</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi></mrow></math></span>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ12"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="d33e657" display="block"><mrow><mi>ρ</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>!</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>!</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>N</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>!</mo></mrow></mfrac><msup><mi>p</mi><mi>i</mi></msup><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>-</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mspace width="4pt"></mspace><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq6"><math id="d33e718"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="double-struck">N</mi></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq7"><math id="d33e731"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi></mrow></math></span> with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq8"><math id="d33e744"><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>&lt;</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span> are given beforehand. We introduce following notation for the Krawtchouk polynomials: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq9"><math id="d33e759"><mrow><msubsup><mi>K</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, here <em>n</em> is the order of the polynomial and <em>p</em> plays the role of a coefficient of asymetry. To compute Krawtchouk polynomials we can use the following recurrent relation (Vainerman, <a href="#CR65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR65">1992</a>):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ13"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="d33e795" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd <p>with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq10"><math id="d33e989"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></math></span>.</p>
<p id="Par13">The orthogonality relation for the Krawtchouk polynomials reads</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ14"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="d33e1009" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><msubsup><mi>K</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><msubsup><mi>K</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mi>ρ</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>δ</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">mn</mi></mrow></msub><mi>N</mi><mo>!</mo><mfrac><mrow><msup><mi>p</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>-</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>!</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>N</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>!</mo></mrow></mfrac><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p>For applications the property of symmetry is important:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ1"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="d33e1109" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msubsup><mi>K</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><msubsup><mi>K</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>-</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>N</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>

</tr></table>
<p>By analogy with the Hermite functions, the corresponding Krawtchouk functions are determined as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ2"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="d33e1174" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mi>K</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><msqrt><mrow><mi>ρ</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msqrt><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><msqrt><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>!</mo><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>p</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>-</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>!</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>N</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>!</mo></mrow></msqrt><mo>,</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">2</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq11"><math id="d33e1264"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="double-struck">N</mi></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq12"><math id="d33e1277"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq13"><math id="d33e1291"><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>&lt;</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>, are set in advance (Vainerman, <a href="#CR65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR65">1992</a>) (Fig. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1</a>). Krawtchouk polynomials are orthogonal, but not normalized, however, for Krawtchouk functions we have</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ3"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="d33e1312" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>δ</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">nm</mi></mrow></msub><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">3</td>
</tr></table>
<p>We also obtain a recurrence relation for the orthonormalized Krawtchouk functions <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq14"><math id="d33e1375"><msub><mi>F</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></math></span>:</p>

<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq15"><math id="d33e1652"><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq16"><math id="d33e1669"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq17"><math id="d33e1686"><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>&lt;</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>. In Al-Utaibi et al. (<a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">2021</a>); Jahid et al. (<a href="#CR30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR30">2017</a>); Karmouni et al. (<a href="#CR32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR32">2017</a>); Idan et al. (<a href="#CR28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR28">2020</a>); Yap et al. (<a href="#CR71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR71">2003</a>); Zhang et al. (<a href="#CR76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR76">2010</a>); Abdulhussain et al. (<a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">2018</a>); Asli and Flusser (<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">2014</a>); Venkataramana and Raj (<a href="#CR67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR67">2011</a>) recurrence algorithms for fast computation of the Krawtchouk functions are presented.</p>

<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ4"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="d33e1734" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>-</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>N</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">4</td>
</tr></table>
<p>In Fig. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1</a> the first four Krawtchouk functions <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq18"><math id="d33e1801"><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq19"><math id="d33e1819"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></math></span> are shown for different coefficients of asymmetry <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq20"><math id="d33e1838"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.5</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.9</mn></mrow></math></span>.</p></section><section id="FPar3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 3</h3>
<p id="Par16">Mathematically, the model of the RF is defined by impulse response weight functions. Assuming linearity the response of the RF to any input signal <em>y</em>(<em>i</em>) can be found by convolving it with these weight functions (Mobarhan et al., <a href="#CR47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR47">2018</a>; Lindeberg, <a href="#CR40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR40">2021</a>; Liu et al., <a href="#CR41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR41">2021</a>; Cho &amp; Choi, <a href="#CR13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR13">2014</a>). As mentioned in the previous step we model the weight functions with Krawtchouk functions. The main specific feature of our model is the introduction of the shift operator which we use to parameterize the basis functions. The idea is the following: the system receives a signal <em>y</em>(<em>i</em>) with an impulse component of unkown location <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq25"><math id="d33e1954"><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math></span> as a hidden variable which will be determined later with our algorithm. We calculate the generalized spectral coefficients of the signal <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq26"><math id="d33e1965"><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> related to a set of basis functions <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq27"><math id="d33e1984"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></math></span> for all possible values <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq28"><math id="d33e2027"><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span> of the shift operator. Applying a standard procedure from the theory of image recognition we glue the ends of the visual field. The novelty of our approach lies in the resulting set of linear transformations by using the functions <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq29"><math id="d33e2044"><mrow><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>a</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo></mrow></mmultiscripts><mtext>mod</mtext><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mi>N</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>,</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>&lt;</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>. In this case, we will get the maximum response exactly when the value of the shift <em>a</em> coincides with the hidden value <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq30"><math id="d33e2105"><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math></span> or, more precisely with the temporary location of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq31"><math id="d33e2116"><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> (Fig. <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig2"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11757888_12021_2024_9698_Fig2_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/e3d9c0cb7fc9/12021_2024_9698_Fig2_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO2" height="529" width="762" alt="Fig. 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The inclusion of saccades in the model of the visual system</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par17">In addition, the Krawtchouk functions contain the parameter <em>p</em>, which determines their degree of asymmetry and allows us to find the basis with the best match to the shape of the signal <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq32"><math id="d33e2143"><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>. In the visual system, the shift of the concentric RF (accordingly, basis functions) throughout the visual field can be related to eye saccades. Eye saccades are fast eye movements that provide an oriented shift of the gaze toward the location of an object. By positioning the image of an object on the retinal region of highest acuity - the fovea, the eye saccades increase the number of neurons activated by the presence of the object; a recruitment which likely contributes to improve its identification and localization (Goffart, <a href="#CR21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR21">2017</a>; Sarrabezolles et al., <a href="#CR54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR54">2017</a>) (Fig. <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2</a>). The eye saccades depend on the relative properties of the visual targets (e.g. size and energy), their landing position adjusts to the larger or to the brighter target in the visual field (Heeman et al., <a href="#CR25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR25">2014</a>). Thus, among all possible responses of saccadic eye movements, one can choose the position of RF oriented to the larger or to the brighter target with the maximum energy. We include saccadic eye movements in our visual system model because it makes it possible to separate information about the location of an object from its shape, in other words it ensures the invariance of object recognition regardless of its location (Fig. <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2</a>). The site of saccadic suppression is likely to be the LGN (Ghodrati et al., <a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">2017</a>), which agrees completely with our model. We assume that saccades are not so much suppressed in the LGN as they are used to determine the position of an object, which exhausts their role in the processing of visual information.</p>
<p id="Par18">Hence we end up with generalized spectral coefficients <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq33"><math id="d33e2183"><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> of the following form</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ16"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="d33e2212" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi>y</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>a</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo></mrow></mmultiscripts><mtext>mod</mtext><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mi>N</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p>here <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq34"><math id="d33e2293"><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>,</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>&lt;</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>. Here, with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq35"><math id="d33e2321"><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo></mrow></math></span> we refer to the discrete convolution.</p></section><section id="FPar4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 4</h3>
<p id="Par19">Krawtchouk functions form orthonormal sequences. The idea is to determine the signal <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq36"><math id="d33e2336"><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> as a finite linear combination of Krawtchouk functions with suitable values <em>a</em> and <em>p</em>. To this end we define the energy functional</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ5"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="d33e2362" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">5</td>
</tr></table>
<p>According to Bessel’s inequality we have</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ6"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="d33e2430" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>≤</mo><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">6</td>
</tr></table>
<p>In Eq. <a href="#Equ6" class="usa-link">6</a> equality holds if and only if <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq37"><math id="d33e2492"><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> belongs to a linear envelope of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq38"><math id="d33e2511"><mrow><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo></mrow></mmultiscripts><mtext>mod</mtext><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mi>N</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. Since <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq39"><math id="d33e2557"><mrow><msup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> is nonnegative Eq. <a href="#Equ5" class="usa-link">5</a> and bounded Eq. <a href="#Equ6" class="usa-link">6</a> its supremum exists and will be attained when the variable parameters will coincide with the hidden ones.</p></section><section id="FPar5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 5</h3>
<p id="Par20">We determine a subspace <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq40"><math id="d33e2594"><mi mathvariant="script">K</mi></math></span> of dimension <em>K</em>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq41"><math id="d33e2607"><mrow><mi>K</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></math></span>, which is generated by the functions with the biggest spectral coefficients. In this step we can essentially limit the number of functions, since we only want to find the location of the signal. Here we simulate the process of saccadic eye movement to find the position of the image of the visual system in the field of view. At first, the visual system approximates the object and in a second step the object will be focused and considered in more detail. In this step we find the low spatial frequencies (the ’fuzzier’ parts) of the image to understand where the image is. In the next step we will find the higher spatial frequencies, the fine details of an image. For the low frequencies we determine some bound <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq42"><math id="d33e2619"><mrow><mi>r</mi><mo>,</mo><mspace width="4pt"></mspace><mi>r</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi></mrow></math></span> for the spectral coefficients <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq43"><math id="d33e2635"><msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>≥</mo><mi>r</mi><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="script">K</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> and build the energy functional</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ7"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="d33e2678" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><munder><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="script">K</mi></mrow></munder><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">7</td>
</tr></table>
<p>We find the supremum of the energy functional</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ8"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="d33e2746" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msub><mtext>sup</mtext><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></msub><msup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">8</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The hidden value of the shift <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq44"><math id="d33e2824"><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math></span> is the value where the energy functional reaches its supremum. This location corresponds to the location of those RF which have maximum response. It gives the information about the location of the signal. The coefficient of asymmetry <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq45"><math id="d33e2835"><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math></span> adjusts the shape of the Krawtchouk functions in the algorithm to the ratio between the front and back of the signal, and thus gives the information about the form of the signal. These two information about the location and form of the signal have been separated.</p></section><section id="FPar6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 6</h3>
<p id="Par21">In the interactive mode with feedback, in each step we find the optimal set of generalized spectral coefficients <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq46"><math id="d33e2851"><mi mathvariant="script">K</mi></math></span> with the dimension <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq47"><math id="d33e2861"><mrow><mi>K</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></math></span>, which provide the restoration error <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq48"><math id="d33e2873"><mi>ε</mi></math></span> we set in advance. In this step, we simulate the feedback of the visual cortex to the LGN. The function of massive feedback projections from the striate cortex to the LGN, far superior to direct retinal projections, remains an unsolved mystery (Ghodrati et al., <a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">2017</a>). An explanation has been proposed that corticothalamic feedback improves feature recognition (Sillito et al., <a href="#CR58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR58">1994</a>; Zabbah et al., <a href="#CR73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR73">2014</a>). In Martinez-Canada et al. (<a href="#CR45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR45">2018</a>) it was shown that in the presence of feedback the maximum RF of LGN response occurs to smaller stimuli, i.e. it is accompanied by spatial focusing. This explanation of the role of the visual cortex feedback to the LGN is fully consistent with our model, since at this step of the algorithm it allows us to include higher frequency Krawtchouk functions in the signal processing and identify more finer signal structures.</p>
<p id="Par22">We define the restored signal</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ9"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="d33e2898" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msup><mi>y</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>:</mo><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>K</mi></munderover><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo></mrow></mmultiscripts><mtext>mod</mtext><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mspace width="4pt"></mspace><mspace width="4pt"></mspace><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">9</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The restauration error is then <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq49"><math id="d33e3003"><mrow><mrow><mi>ε</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><msup><mi>y</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mrow><mo>-</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq50"><math id="d33e3025"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mo>·</mo><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow></math></span> is the Euclidean norm in <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq51"><math id="d33e3039"><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi></mrow><mi>K</mi></msup></math></span>. Thus, this set of generalized spectral coefficients contains the essential information of the signal form and represents the essential features of the signal. Also, these spectral coefficients are invariant to the hidden shift of the signal because we obtain in addition the shift <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq52"><math id="d33e3052"><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math></span> and the coefficient of asymmetry <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq53"><math id="d33e3064"><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math></span> and accordingly, the information of the form of the signal has been separated from its location.</p>
<p id="Par23">In Usrey and Alitto (<a href="#CR63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR63">2015</a>) it is shown that visual processing in the LGN includes spatial and temporal influences on visual signals that serve to adjust response gain, transform the temporal structure of retinal activity patterns, and increase the signal-to-noise ratio of the retinal signal while preserving its basic content. At the same time in Graumann et al. (<a href="#CR23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR23">2022</a>) it was found that location representations of the objects emerge along the ventral visual stream towards the lateral occipital complex. Their calculation involves recurrent processing in the high-level visual cortex. Our model is consistent with these results and allows us to assume an important role of the LGN in the recursive process of finding both informative features of objects and their location by the visual system. As usual models of technical systems are robust, but this model of a biological system is sensitive to small details of the image. As eyes can see small image features so also our proposed algorithm can extract the corresponding features. This property can be useful, for example, in automated ECG diagnostic systems, since the small details such as “little plateaus”, “little jags” etc. are significant for diagnosis, but most existing algorithms smooth them out. The problem of model robustness is solved on the stage of decision making: the investigator decides to take these details into account or to reject them as noise.</p>
<p id="Par24">A convolutional neural network (CNN) was recently developed as a model of the visual system, where each neuron processes the input signal exclusively within its receptive field (Rakhmatulin et al., <a href="#CR51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR51">2024</a>). Consequently, our proposed method can be naturally understood within the framework of CNNs. These networks have been successfully applied to EEG analysis for recognizing various human states, including depression, neurocognitive dysfunctions, emotional states, and motor imagery for brain-computer interfaces. Additionally, CNNs have proven effective in decoding task-related information directly from raw EEG data without relying on handcrafted features (Rajwal &amp; Aggarwal, <a href="#CR50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR50">2023</a>; Schirrmeister et al., <a href="#CR55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR55">2017</a>; Rakhmatulin et al., <a href="#CR51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR51">2024</a>). A typical CNN architecture consists of three layers: the Convolutional Layer, the Pooling Layer and the Fully-Connected Layer (Rajwal &amp; Aggarwal, <a href="#CR50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR50">2023</a>). In the Convolutional Layer, the input signal is convolved with weight functions that model the responses (filters) of receptive fields, enabling the determination of the significant signal features for subsequent identification and classification. This feature extraction process is a critical step in EEG signal analysis, as it transforms raw data into numerical features suitable for further processing (Rakhmatulin et al., <a href="#CR51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR51">2024</a>). The quality of these features significantly impacts the signal identification and the classification efficiency in subsequent CNN layers. Consequently, this study focuses on identifying informative signal features that are shift-invariant and independ of the signal’s starting point. These informative signal features can be applied across various classification methods, though their analysis is beyond the scope of this article. As highlighted in Rajwal and Aggarwal (<a href="#CR50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR50">2023</a>), certain challenges arise when using CNNs for EEG analysis. At first, CNNs require large amount of training data. Given that EEG recordings often have varying starting points and are inherently non-stationary signals, the potential size of the training dataset becomes virtually infinite. Our proposed method extracts invariant features, which can help reduce the size of the required training set. Another challenge in using CNNs for EEG analysis is overfitting, where the model becomes too closely tailored to the training data. compromising its ability to generalize to unseen data (Rajwal &amp; Aggarwal, <a href="#CR50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR50">2023</a>). In our approach, the training sequence is utilized to determine the threshold for the maximum energy functional, which serves as the pooling mode. This threshold determines the accuracy of signal recovery, effectively defining the required level of signal detail. According to the maximum energy functional value, only features with amplitudes exceeding a certain threshold are considered significant. While higher-order frequencies typically exhibit lower amplitudes than low frequencies, they are crucial for capturing the more minor signal details. Consequently, increasing the number of Krawtchouk functions in the CNN Convolution Layer enhances the model’s ability to generalize to unseen data. Importantly, our approach does not rely on predefined signal features from the training set, but adapts the set of filters in the Convolution Layer to the signal shape. Importantly, our approach does not rely on predefined signal features from the training set to adapt the model to a new input signal. Instead, the Convolution Layer’s filters are dynamically adjusted to match the shape of the input signal. This key distinction from existing methods helps mitigate overfitting, as the training process is designed to be universal for signals of a specific type, independent of the exact values of the training features. Another challenge in using CNNs is the complexity of interpretation (Rajwal &amp; Aggarwal, <a href="#CR50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR50">2023</a>). Typically, it is unclear what specific features a CNN utilizes for classification. In our proposed method, however, the set of informative features is entirely transparent, consisting of signal decomposition coefficients derived from Krawtchouk basis functions. These functions are optimally adapted to the localization of the signal’s impulse components and its asymmetry.</p>
<p id="Par25">In our opinion, a more significant issue with applying CNNs to EEG signals is their non-stationary nature (Rajwal &amp; Aggarwal, <a href="#CR50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR50">2023</a>). In the CNN algorithm, the Convolution Layer processes the input signal within a fixed window, convolving it with filter weight functions across all frequencies. If the processing window is determined by the wavelength of the lowest frequency, higher frequencies are also confined to this window. As a result, high-frequency components, which may undergo multiple oscillations within the window, can exhibit time-varying dynamics that the CNN algorithm cannot effectively capture. While CNNs excel at visual image analysis (Rakhmatulin et al., <a href="#CR51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR51">2024</a>), they are not inherently designed to analyze signal dynamics. To address this limitation, we incorporated wavelet analysis into our approach. At the next stage, we developed a method that combines a Convolutional Layer for extracting invariant signal features with signal analysis performed within wavelet analysis windows of varying scales.</p></section></section><section id="Sec3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Algorithm for Determining the Temporal Localization of the Impulse Components of the Signal and their Frequency Characteristics</h2>
<p id="Par26">The ideology of the wavelet transform is to consequently decompose a signal with some impulse functions in different scales (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a>). In each cell of this scheme we will use an algorithm based on the model presented above (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4</a>). We propose a wavelet transform with Krawtchouk functions as the mother-wavelet, based on ideas from image recognition. The input of the system receives a signal <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq54"><math id="d33e3138"><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> with an impulse component localized in the short duration of the continuous signal which corresponds to some reaction of the process under study. We will discuss what is meant by the reaction in Step 7 of the next algorithm. The task is to find the reaction time (the time of the impulse component) and determine its frequency characteristics.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig3"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 3.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11757888_12021_2024_9698_Fig3_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/fc043644dd03/12021_2024_9698_Fig3_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO3" height="383" width="708" alt="Fig. 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Signal wavelet transformation scheme</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig4"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 4.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11757888_12021_2024_9698_Fig4_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/a8fe9c3bdc62/12021_2024_9698_Fig4_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO4" height="1076" width="681" alt="Fig. 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The algorithm of signal processing</p></figcaption></figure><section id="FPar7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 1</h3>
<p id="Par27">We define the scale of the wavelet transform with window widths of <em>N</em> points, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq55"><math id="d33e3171"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>9</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo></mrow></math></span> We begin from window widths of 1024 points because we use this algorithm to analize the EEG which was recorded with a discrete frequency of 500 Hz and with filters of high and low frequencies respectively with 0,5 Hz and 200 Hz.</p></section><section id="FPar8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 2</h3>
<p id="Par28">We create a new zero level of the input signal in the biggest window: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq56"><math id="d33e3201"><mrow><mi>y</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">input</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>-</mo><mover><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">input</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1024</mn></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span> where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq57"><math id="d33e3265"><mrow><mover><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">input</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>=</mo><mo>∑</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">input</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><mn>1024</mn></mrow></math></span> is the average of illumination for the biggest window.</p></section><section id="FPar9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 3</h3>
<p id="Par29">We calculate the first four Krawtchouk functions for the windows <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq58"><math id="d33e3316"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>9</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq59"><math id="d33e3341"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.2</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>0.9</mn></mrow></math></span>, since we use 9 asymmetry coefficients of the Krawtchouk functions for a better approximation of non-symmetrical impulse components. Since the functions are symmetric Eq. <a href="#Equ4" class="usa-link">4</a>, we only need to calculate the Krawtchouk functions for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq60"><math id="d33e3362"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.2</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.3</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.4</mn></mrow></math></span> and for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq61"><math id="d33e3380"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.6</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.7</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.8</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.9</mn></mrow></math></span>. At <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq62"><math id="d33e3399"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.5</mn></mrow></math></span> the Krawtchouk functions are symmetric w.r.t. the middle of the interval, therefore, they are calculated only for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq63"><math id="d33e3411"><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span>. This reduces the accumulation of the error in the recurrent calculation of the following function values. Fast algorithms for the computation of the Krawtchouk functions can be found in Al-Utaibi et al. (<a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">2021</a>); Jahid et al. (<a href="#CR30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR30">2017</a>); Venkataramana and Raj (<a href="#CR67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR67">2011</a>); Yap et al. (<a href="#CR71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR71">2003</a>); Zhang et al. (<a href="#CR76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR76">2010</a>); Abdulhussain et al. (<a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">2018</a>); Asli and Flusser (<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">2014</a>). In addition, we can calculate the function values in advance and save them in the corresponding file. In each window we use the first four Krawtchouk functions, since numerical experiments which analyze various signals have shown that this is the optimal number of functions. The use of a smaller number of functions does not allow a sufficient analysis of the shape of complex impulse components, and a larger number of functions captures the frequencies of the next level. Also, the size of the last smallest window is four points. Thus, the four Krawtchouk functions in the last window constitute a complete set of basis functions, which makes it possible to carry out an exhaustive analysis of the signal and avoid losses.</p></section><section id="FPar10"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 4</h3>
<p id="Par30">We calculate the generalized spectral coefficients relative to the set of shifts of the basis of Krawtchouk functions: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq64"><math id="d33e3458"><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi>y</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>a</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo></mrow></mmultiscripts><mtext>mod</mtext><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq65"><math id="d33e3532"><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>;</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>;</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.2</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>0.9</mn></mrow></math></span>. To calculate the generalized spectral coefficients, we use the convolution theorem</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ17"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="d33e3573" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mi>y</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>a</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="script">F</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi mathvariant="script">F</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>y</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>·</mo><mi mathvariant="script">F</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>a</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq66"><math id="d33e3704"><mi mathvariant="script">F</mi></math></span> is the Fourier transformation, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq67"><math id="d33e3714"><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>,</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.2</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>0.9</mn></mrow></math></span>. We keep in mind that the ends of the window of view have been glued.</p></section><section id="FPar11"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 5</h3>
<p id="Par31">We calculate the energy functional</p>
