<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ18"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="d33e3755" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><munder><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></munder><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td></tr></table></section><section id="FPar12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 6</h3>
<p id="Par32">We find the maximum of the energy functional</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ19"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="d33e3830" display="block"><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>0.9</mn><mo>;</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></msub><msup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">max</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>.</mo></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p>At this step, we find the temporal localization of the impulse component of the signal and the asymmetry coefficients of the Krawtchouk basis functions, which maximally correspond to the asymmetry of the impulse. Convolutional neural networks are very effectively used to analyze and classify EEG, for example, in Lo Giudice et al. (<a href="#CR43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR43">2023</a>). In contrast to convolutional neural networks, we use a convolution of the input signal with a set of bases, which is parameterized by the shift and asymmetry parameters rather than a single basis. At this step, we find the unique basis in which the impulse has a response with the highest energy.</p></section><section id="FPar13"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 7</h3>
<p id="Par33">Now we determine whether the extremum found in the previous step has to be interpreted as a “reaction” or as background fluctuations. If <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq68"><math id="d33e3912"><mrow><msubsup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">max</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>≥</mo><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq69"><math id="d33e3950"><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></math></span> is the threshold value of the energy functional for the window of level <em>n</em>, we define this part of the signal as reaction and go to the next step. The threshold values <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq70"><math id="d33e3964"><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></math></span> are found in advance using a training sequence. This training algorithm of classifiers finds the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq71"><math id="d33e3975"><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>i</mi><msub><mi>n</mi><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>D</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></msub><msubsup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq72"><math id="d33e4030"><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>D</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span> is a set of signals with an impulse component, which the experts define as a “reaction”, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq73"><math id="d33e4047"><mrow><msubsup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> are the energy functionals of the “reaction”. In the case <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq74"><math id="d33e4084"><mrow><msubsup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">max</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> we proceed to signal processing inside the windows of the next level.</p></section><section id="FPar14"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 8</h3>
<p id="Par34">We determine the time of reaction (impulse) in window number <em>l</em> of level number <em>n</em>: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq75"><math id="d33e4132"><mrow><msubsup><mi>a</mi><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq76"><math id="d33e4152"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq77"><math id="d33e4170"><mrow><mi>l</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span> and find the amplitudes and frequencies of reaction (impulse) in this window: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq78"><math id="d33e4190"><msubsup><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mn>4</mn></msubsup></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq79"><math id="d33e4229"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>.</p></section><section id="FPar15"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 9</h3>
<p id="Par35">We restore the impulse component of the signal</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ10"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="d33e4252" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">~</mo></mover><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><munder><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></munder><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo></mrow></mmultiscripts><mtext>mod</mtext><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">10</td>
</tr></table></section><section id="FPar16"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 10</h3>
<p id="Par36">We subtract the reconstructed signal from the input signal: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq80"><math id="d33e4362"><mrow><msubsup><mi>y</mi><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mi>y</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>-</mo><mover accent="true"><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">~</mo></mover><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>;</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>. Thus, we consider oscillations at low frequencies as drift of the isoline for the oscillations of high frequencies. In addition, this eliminates the repeated finding of the frequency characteristics of the same impulse component in the next windows of the wavelet analysis. This approach is also one of the distinguishing features of our algorithm compared to other wavelet transform algorithms.</p></section><section id="FPar17"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 11</h3>
<p id="Par37">We go to the next window <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq81"><math id="d33e4414"><mrow><mi>l</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>l</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span> at this level <em>n</em> of wavelet analysis. If the rest of the signal is less than the window width, we pad the signal with zeros: if <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq82"><math id="d33e4431"><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>·</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></math></span>, then <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq83"><math id="d33e4456"><mrow><mi>y</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>. In case we have reached the end of the sequence of windows of a given level <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq84"><math id="d33e4489"><mrow><mi>l</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, we go to the next level of windows <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq85"><math id="d33e4504"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>. In the case <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq86"><math id="d33e4518"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span> the algorithm reaches the end.</p>
<p id="Par38">When estimating the time complexity of an algorithm, only the component with the fastest growth rate is considered. In the case of our algorithm, the most computationally expensive operations are the recursive calculations involved in generating the Krawtchouk functions. To optimize performance, we precomputed these functions and their Fourier transforms, storing them in corresponding files for reuse. The next most significant contributors to time complexity are typically loop executions and procedure calls. For our algorithm, this includes the convolution computations, which we optimized by employing the Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) procedure to reduce computational overhead. We used the Cooley-Tukey FFT algorithm, which has a time complexity of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq87"><math id="d33e4532"><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>N</mi><msub><mo>log</mo><mn>2</mn></msub><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq88"><math id="d33e4551"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></math></span> (with <em>n</em> representing the maximum power of 2 defining the largest scale in wavelet anaysis) is the length of the largest wavelet analysis window.</p>
<p id="Par40">The next most computationally intensive component is the set of nested loops in our algorithm. Specifically, our algorithm employs five nested loops, resulting in a time complexity of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq89"><math id="d33e4583"><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>·</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>·</mo><mi>p</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>·</mo><mi>N</mi><mo>·</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>. Here, <em>n</em>1 represents the number of scale levels, <em>p</em>1 is the number of asymmetry coefficient values <em>p</em> for the Krawtchouk functions, <em>N</em> is the length of the largest window, and the second <em>N</em> corresponds to the number of shifts applied to the Krawtchouk functions. For instance, EEG data was recorded at a sampling rate of 500 Hz, using a high-pass filter at 0.5 Hz and a low-pass filter at 200 Hz. For analysis, we employed windows with a maximum length of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq90"><math id="d33e4625"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1024</mn><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mn>10</mn></msup></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq91"><math id="d33e4641"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>, with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq92"><math id="d33e4657"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>=</mo><mn>9</mn></mrow></math></span> asymmetry coefficients <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq93"><math id="d33e4670"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.2</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>0.9</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>. The first four Krawtchouk functionswere utilized at each of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq94"><math id="d33e4695"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>=</mo><mn>9</mn></mrow></math></span> scale levels <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq95"><math id="d33e4709"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>9</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>. When calculating the complexity, constant multipliers can be disregarded, simplifying the algorithm’s complexity to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq96"><math id="d33e4734"><mrow><mi>O</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>N</mi><msub><mo>log</mo><mn>2</mn></msub><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>+</mo><mi>O</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>N</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. Based on wavelet analysis principles, long signals are processed sequentially in windows of maximum size. As a result, the algorithm demonstrates manageable complexity and is well-suited for real-time EEG analysis, even with larger datasets.</p>
<p id="Par41">For comparison, we outline the time complexities of the CNN method and wavelet analysis. For a CNN architecture, the algorithm’s complexity can be approximated as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq97"><math id="d33e4766"><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>·</mo><mi>N</mi><mo>·</mo><mi>M</mi><mo>·</mo><mi>n</mi><msup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>L</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mo>·</mo><mi>n</mi><msup><mi>F</mi><mi>L</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>, where <em>k</em> is the size of the kernel matrix, <em>N</em> and <em>M</em> are the number of rows and columns of the input matrix, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq98"><math id="d33e4808"><mrow><mi>n</mi><msup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>L</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span> represents the number of filters in the preceding CNN layer and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq99"><math id="d33e4825"><mrow><mi>n</mi><msup><mi>F</mi><mi>L</mi></msup></mrow></math></span> is the number of filters in the current layer (Rusnac &amp; Grigore, <a href="#CR53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR53">2022</a>). In contrast, the time complexity of the wavelet transformation for a signal of length <em>N</em> is <em>O</em>(<em>N</em>) (Amin et al., <a href="#CR4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR4">2020</a>).</p></section></section><section id="Sec4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Experimental Results</h2>
<section id="Sec5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Example N1</h3>
<p id="Par42">When studying the processes of attention, it is important to be able to analyze the moment of blinking. As a rule, in this case, minima are found in the electrical activity records of the brain in the prefrontal leads. However, muscle artifacts may be present in these recordings. Currently, they are found and removed from further analysis manually. We applied the developed method to find the moments of blinking and will use it in future to recognize and remove the muscle artifacts automatically from further analysis. Figure <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5</a> a) shows the moments of blinking on the scales of 1024 and 512 points. The EEG was recorded with the sampling rate of 500 Hz, so the blinking moments are defined at 346 points = 653 ms and 513 points = 1026 ms. Thus, blinking was identified in the range of 0.5 - 1 Hz, and the muscle artifact was identified in the range of 16 Hz (32 points).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig5"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/c26023ed22bf/12021_2024_9698_Fig5_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO5" height="1026" width="706" alt="Fig. 5"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The fragment of an EEG recording in the lead Fp1 a) with the events of blinking and a muscle artifact and b) its restorations according to the formula Eq. <a href="#Equ10" class="usa-link">10</a></p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par43">The proposed method allowed not only the calculation of the exact time coordinates of different impulse components but also their frequency characteristics, which were used to reconstruct the corresponding impulses according to the formula Eq. <a href="#Equ10" class="usa-link">10</a> (Fig. <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5</a> b)). This proves that our hypothesis about the method of integrating the model of extraction of shift-invariant signal features into wavelet analysis is confirmed. Thus, the calculated generalized spectral coefficients contain information about the impulse shape. They can be further used to identify and classify different impulses and decide whether to remove or include them in subsequent analyses. For example, blinks can be used to study attention processes or simply removed from the EEG signal.</p>
<p id="Par44">We performed a statistical analysis comparing the positions of the maximum energy and signal extremes functions for 25 different EEG recordings with localized impulses. A distribution of the absolute values of the difference between the maximum energy and signal extremes was significant differing from the normal distribution (Shapiro-Wilk’s test <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq100"><math id="d33e4872"><mrow><mi>W</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.432514</mn></mrow></math></span>; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq101"><math id="d33e4884"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>0.001</mn></mrow></math></span>). In this case, a valid estimate of central tendency is the median Me, which here is Me <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq102"><math id="d33e4896"><mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>8</mn></mrow></math></span> points, and the variance is represented by the interquartile range [Lower Quartile, Upper Quartile], which is Guger et al. (<a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">2000</a>); Cope et al. (<a href="#CR14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR14">2009</a>) points. The inexact coincidence of the maximum energy functional and the momentum extrema is explained by the fact that the energy functional is a smooth function and the momentum extrema may have local point-like outliers.</p></section><section id="Sec6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Example N2</h3>
<p id="Par45">In a study of human emotional reactions, an EEG was recorded with sampling rate of 500 Hz during the presentation of pictures. The pictures were randomly selected from a database of standardized sets of pictures, the International Affective Picture System (IAPS). In addition to brain activity, the researchers were interested in muscle reactions in certain leads upon presentation of some pictures. The task was to find the time, duration and power of the muscle reaction of individual subjects during the presentation of certain pictures.</p>
<p id="Par46">Figure <a href="#Fig6" class="usa-link">6</a> shows the results of processing the EEG record in lead P3 when viewing a certain picture in one subject. The muscle response was found in the range of scales 512 - 16 points (1 - 31 Hz) between the points 587 - 736 (1174 - 1472 ms). Thus, the duration was 298 ms with a power of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq103"><math id="d33e4922"><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>9</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>5</mn></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>,</mo><mi>l</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></msub><msubsup><mi>W</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mi>l</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>1979384</mn><mspace width="4pt"></mspace><msup><mtext>mkV</mtext><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></math></span>. This makes it possible to compare the presence/absence of a muscle reaction in a given subject to various pictures and the presence/absence of a typical muscle reaction in other subjects to similar pictures.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig6"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/1a46f22a5375/12021_2024_9698_Fig6_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO6" height="1301" width="714" alt="Fig. 6"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The fragment of an EEG recording in the lead P3 with a) the muscle reaction when viewing an emotional picture, b) the localization of the muscle reaction - the restored signal by the corresponding generalized spectral coefficients in different frequencies according to the formula Eq. <a href="#Equ10" class="usa-link">10</a> and c) its wavelet spectrum</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par47">It is noteworthy that the proposed method made it possible to calculate not only the time parameters of the muscle reaction but also the frequencies, which further opens up the possibility of determining the features of both individual and typical emotional reactions.</p></section><section id="Sec7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Example N3</h3>
<p id="Par48">Removal of the artifacts from EEG is a large topic that requires detailed discussion. We plan to demonstrate the capabilities of our method for removing artifacts of different types in EEG in the next article. Now we would like to highlight that certain artifacts can carry additional useful information. Usually, the researcher is not able to analyze artifacts, because existing methods reject them and the researcher analyzes only the clean EEG in the next step. In the following example, we want to demonstrate that decomposing the initial EEG into artifact and clean EEG can provide additional possibilities for analyzing human brain activity.</p>
<p id="Par49">In this example, we consider a study in which the Choice Reaction was examined. In this case, the EEG was labeled “stimulus” during stimulus presentation, and the EEG was labeled “answer” when a button was pressed during the person’s response. As can be seen from Fig. <a href="#Fig7" class="usa-link">7</a> blinking is often associated with the moment of response. This may be a demonstration of a generalized motor response that may be of interest in BCI studies.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig7"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/15764b3829e0/12021_2024_9698_Fig7_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO7" height="583" width="720" alt="Fig. 7"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The fragment of an EEG recording during the Choice Reaction was examined</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par50">In Fig. <a href="#Fig8" class="usa-link">8</a> we have presented the decomposition components of EEG recordings in frontal leads in the largest window of wavelet analysis of 1024 points. The thresholds were chosen in such a way as to extract blinks in the largest window of wavelet analysis and find temporal parameters of their localization. Some blinks were lost, for example in lead F4, because their amplitude was not enough to cross the corresponding threshold. But we find this impulse on a different scale - in a 27 = 128-point window. This example shows the difficulties we have with finding a balance between the thresholds in different scales. But this example also demonstrates the potential of the method for studying synchronization processes in brain activity during test tasks.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig8"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/3f7c37356f27/12021_2024_9698_Fig8_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO8" height="789" width="712" alt="Fig. 8"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The fragment of an EEG recording during the Choice Reaction was examined</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Example N4</h3>
<p id="Par51">In EEG analysis tasks, it is often necessary to separate the signal from the background noise. In the following example, we will demonstrate the capabilities of the method to extract the impulse component in Fp1A1. Using the impulse component, we will understand an impulse associated with blinking. The extraction of such a component may be of interest in the tasks of BCI systems. Figure <a href="#Fig9" class="usa-link">9</a> a) shows the original signal-EEG recording in the Fp1A1 lead with the overlaid component, which was extracted in a window of 1024 points according to formula (9). Figure <a href="#Fig9" class="usa-link">9</a> b) shows the noise graph. Note that the impulse component was extracted despite the presence of concomitant high-frequency and high-amplitude noise. Noise we define as sum of components in the windows from 4 to 512 points. In the Fig. <a href="#Fig10" class="usa-link">10</a> we present the decomposition of initial signal in all wavelet windows from 1024 points to 4. The graphics of the components, which were restored in the windows from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq104"><math id="d33e5043"><mrow><msup><mn>2</mn><mn>8</mn></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>256</mn></mrow></math></span> to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq105"><math id="d33e5057"><mrow><msup><mn>2</mn><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></math></span> we presented with the shift in the y axis for better visualization. The energy of the extracted signal was calculated using the formula <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq106"><math id="d33e5072"><mrow><mi>E</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mtext>W-10</mtext><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mn>1024</mn></msubsup><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mn>4</mn></msubsup><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>193050614</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>065</mn></mrow></math></span>. The noise energy was calculated using the formula <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq107"><math id="d33e5123"><mrow><mi>E</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mtext>noise</mtext><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><mn>9</mn></msubsup><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mn>1024</mn></msubsup><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mn>4</mn></msubsup><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mi>j</mi><mi>k</mi></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>204026625</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>049</mn></mrow></math></span>. So, the signal/noise ratio is 0.95. Let us define the distance between the initial signal and the sum of the impulse component and noise as the maximum of the absolute difference of ordinates, then it is equal to 0.00001. It proves that all information without any losses is contained in the components of the wavelet decomposition, and the researcher can decide for himself what information should be saved and analyzed in the future and what should be rejected.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig9"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/079fd28e244a/12021_2024_9698_Fig9_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO9" height="1150" width="764" alt="Fig. 9"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig9/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>a) Initial EEG recorded in Fp1A1 and the first component of the wavelet analysis of it in the largest window of 1024 points W-10, which was calculated according to the formula (9); b) the noise</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig10"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 10.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11757888_12021_2024_9698_Fig10_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/df40f2cc1b3f/12021_2024_9698_Fig10_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO10" height="496" width="686" alt="Fig. 10"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig10/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Initial EEG recorded in Fp1A1 and its decomposition in all wavelet windows from 1024 points to 4</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Example N5</h3>
<p id="Par52">We determined the physiological price paid by the organism during cognitive load based on the analysis of the electrocardiogram (ECG) recorded during the test tasks. The goal was to determine the degree of tension in the regulatory mechanisms of the human organism. To do this, we calculated the stress index (SI) according to Baevsky, which determines the ratio of activity of the sympathetic and parasympathetic divisions of the autonomic nervous system (Baevsky &amp; Chernikova, <a href="#CR7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR7">2017</a>).</p>
<p id="Par53">The first step was to determine the heart rhythm based on the initial ECG. As shown in Fig. <a href="#Fig11" class="usa-link">11</a>, the first component of the wavelet analysis of the initial ECG in the largest window of 1024 points, which was calculated according to the formula (9), allowed us to determine the location of the R wave of the QRS complex with an accuracy of 1 point. Since we recorded the ECG with a sampling rate of 1000 Hz, the accuracy of determining the R-R intervals was 0.001-0.002 s.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig11"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 11.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/b4f81a173b70/12021_2024_9698_Fig11_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO11" height="644" width="706" alt="Fig. 11"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig11/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Initial ECG and the first component of the wavelet analysis of it in the largest window of 1024 points, which was calculated according to the formula (9)</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par54">The dynamics of the SI for a car driver was studied. Test recordings of the ECG were carried out in collaboration with TROUT GmbH (<a href="#CR62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR62">2022</a>) in participants while passing specific modes of the car driving simulator. On the basis of ECG analysis, the SI was calculated in dynamics with a shift of one second. The task was to determine the time of change in the driver’s state by the level of his SI. Since SI has many outlying points and numerous local maxima, it was difficult for us to determine the threshold that would indicate a permanently elevated level of SI. Therefore, we started looking for a parameter that would be smoother, correlated well with SI, and that we could analyze in run-time. We used the wavelet analysis for the SI and its wavelet spectrum is shown in Fig. <a href="#Fig12" class="usa-link">12</a> with windows SI<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq108"><math id="d33e5210"><mi>_</mi></math></span>1 with 1024 points, SI<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq109"><math id="d33e5219"><mi>_</mi></math></span>2 with 512 points, ..., SI<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq110"><math id="d33e5228"><mi>_</mi></math></span>5 with 64 points. The SI variables and amplitudes of the wavelet spectra had a distribution which is significantly different from Gaussian (according to Lilliefors test <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq111"><math id="d33e5238"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow></math></span>), so we evaluated their interrelation by the Spearman correlation coefficient. The SI correlated with the first component of the 1024-point window wavelet spectrum, which we calculated according the formula (7), with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq112"><math id="d33e5250"><mrow><msub><mi>r</mi><mi>s</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.84</mn></mrow></math></span>. On this basis, we could find times of changes in the driver’s state during the test with different stress loads.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig12"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 12.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/cea4de2da18a/12021_2024_9698_Fig12_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO12" height="965" width="717" alt="Fig. 12"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig12/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The stress index of the driver during the test on the simulator with different levels of the stress load, the first component of the 1024-point window wavelet spectrum WKr-10<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq113"><math id="d33e5277"><mi>_</mi></math></span>SI, which was calculated according to the formula (9) and the wavelet spectrum of SI</p></figcaption></figure></section></section><section id="Sec10"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Wavelet Analysis with Krawtchouk Functions as Mother Wavelet</h2>
<p id="Par55">We want to show the difference between the proposed method, which we will call wavelet analysis for localization, and the classical wavelet analysis, which uses Krawtchouk functions as the mother wavelet. Let us consider the wavelet analysis method where the mother wavelet is defined by Krawtchouk functions. According to Walnut (<a href="#CR69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR69">2002</a>), the wavelet analysis algorithm consists of the following steps.</p>
<section id="FPar18"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 1</h3>
<p id="Par56">We define the scale of the wavelet transform with the windows of length of <em>N</em> points, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq114"><math id="d33e5302"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow></math></span>. In the wavelet analysis method using invariance with respect to shift proposed above, which we will call wavelet analysis for localization, we used the first four Krawtchouk functions in each analysis window. Therefore, in this algorithm of wavelet analysis without invariance, which we will call wavelet analysis, we will also use the first four Krawtchouk functions in each window. For the first four Krawtchouk functions, we define the scaled and translated basis functions <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq115"><math id="d33e5327"><mrow><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>;</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span> for the windows <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq116"><math id="d33e5365"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>⋯</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq117"><math id="d33e5390"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.5</mn></mrow></math></span>. The parameter <em>n</em> defines the scale (or dilation), parameter <em>b</em> the location (shift, translation) of the wavelets which changes discretely proportional to the n-th degree of the wavelet scale <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq118"><math id="d33e5409"><mrow><mi>b</mi><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo><mi>s</mi></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq119"><math id="d33e5426"><mrow><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>s</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mrow><mn>10</mn><mo>-</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>, then <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq120"><math id="d33e5456"><mrow><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>b</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1024</mn></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>.</p></section><section id="FPar19"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 2</h3>
<p id="Par57">We define a signal processing window with a maximum degree of two, determining the segment of the signal to be analyzed. In this instance, we select the initial segment of the signal, denoted by <em>y</em>(<em>i</em>), with a length of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq121"><math id="d33e5512"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>1024</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow></math></span> points. Subsequently, we proceed to the next segment and continue this process until reaching the end of the signal.</p></section><section id="FPar20"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 3</h3>
<p id="Par58">We start the signal processing with the smallest windows with a width of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq122"><math id="d33e5536"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span> points.</p></section><section id="FPar21"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 4</h3>
<p id="Par59">The wavelet transform is computed by taking the inner product of <em>y</em>(<em>i</em>) with translated and scaled versions of a wavelet, as described in Burrus et al. (<a href="#CR12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR12">1997</a>). The wavelet coefficients are determined through the inner product of signal and wavelet basis functions</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ20"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="d33e5569" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><munder><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></munder><mi>y</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo><msubsup><mi>F</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mover><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow><mo>¯</mo></mover><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td></tr></table></section><section id="FPar22"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 5</h3>
<p id="Par60">We calculate the energy functional</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ21"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="d33e5639" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msup><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><munder><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></munder><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td></tr></table></section><section id="FPar23"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 6</h3>
<p id="Par61">We proceed to the next window with a length of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq123"><math id="d33e5690"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span> points. We repeat steps 4-5 until we reach the end of the maximum-length window, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq124"><math id="d33e5708"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1024</mn><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow></math></span>.</p></section><section id="FPar24"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 7</h3>
<p id="Par62">We repeat steps 4-5 for windows with a width of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq125"><math id="d33e5732"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>8</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></math></span> points until we reach the end of the maximum-length window <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq126"><math id="d33e5752"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1024</mn><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow></math></span>. Subsequently, we repeat steps 4-5 for windows with widths of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq127"><math id="d33e5772"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>·</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow></math></span> points.</p></section><section id="FPar25"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 8</h3>
<p id="Par63">We proceed to analyze the next segment of the signal using the window of maximum length, following steps 2-7. If the remaining portion of the signal is smaller than the window width, we pad the signal with zeros. The algorithm is finished when the last segment of the signal has been analyzed.</p>
<p>The majority of studies on EEG wavelet analysis concentrate on methods for classifying EEGs based on their wavelet spectrum (Gosala et al., <a href="#CR22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR22">2023</a>; Subasi, <a href="#CR59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR59">2005</a>; Amin et al., <a href="#CR4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR4">2020</a>; Raghu et al., <a href="#CR49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR49">2019</a>; Zahirovic et al., <a href="#CR74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR74">2021</a>). While a variety of functions are employed as the mother wavelet (Bajaj, <a href="#CR8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR8">2020</a>), in our opinion, insufficient emphasis has been placed on investigating how the properties of these functions influence the accuracy of EEG signal decomposition. It is important to note that the French hat wavelet, Mexican hat wavelet, Morlet wavelet (or Gabor wavelet), and first-order Gaussian wavelet do not constitute a basis in the strict mathematical sense. This means they cannot form a coordinate system suitable for decomposing the original signal. Haar and Walsh wavelets, while forming bases, are periodic signals, which makes their energy functional unsuitable for identifying impulse localization. Daubechies wavelets, on the other hand, provide a discrete basis with an impulse-like shape, but they are complex to construct, and their shift parametrization is not straightforward. The Hermite wavelet is the most suitable wavelet for continuous variables. However, as it is defined over a continuous domain, its implementation in computational algorithms requires discretization. This discretization introduces errors that compromise the orthogonality of Hermite functions as basis functions, resulting in some loss of accuracy in EEG signal decomposition. Furthermore, Hermite functions are inherently symmetric, meaning that representing an asymmetric signal requires combining two components with distinct frequencies, corresponding to the signal’s leading and trailing slopes. Krawtchouk functions are inherently constructed as an orthonormal system with a discrete argument, making them well-suited as a mother wavelet and free from the limitations associated with discretizing continuous functions. Additionally, the inclusion of a parameter to define the asymmetry coefficient enables more precise analysis of asymmetric signals.</p></section></section><section id="Sec11"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Comparison of the Methods</h2>
<p id="Par65">The main difference between the proposed wavelet analysis method for localization and classical wavelet analysis is that in classical wavelet analysis, a single basis function is used in the signal processing window to decompose the signal into the corresponding generalized spectral coefficients (determining the amplitudes of the corresponding frequencies). We use a set of basis functions, which is parameterized by a shift and an asymmetry coefficient. Our approach aims to find the single basis among these basis functions that must conform to the impulse component and to provide that the values of the shift and asymmetry parameters are most appropriate for the corresponding properties of the impulse.</p>
<p id="Par66">In this section we compare the results of signal analysis using wavelet analysis and wavelet analysis for localization, as described in the main part of the article. In these examples, we will denote the energy functionals calculated in the windows with a length of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq128"><math id="d33e5825"><mrow><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span> points according to wavelet analysis as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq129"><math id="d33e5845"><mrow><mi>W</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span> and as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq130"><math id="d33e5865"><mrow><mi>W</mi><mi>K</mi><mi>r</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span> according to wavelet analysis for localization.</p>
<p id="Par67">Figure <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">13</a> displays a segment consisting of 1024 points from the EEG recorded in the Fp2 lead, featuring a blink artifact along with its wavelet analysis. In Fig. <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">13</a> a), the signal is presented with the superimposed energy functional <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq131"><math id="d33e5896"><mrow><mi>W</mi><mi>K</mi><mi>r</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow></math></span> in a window of length 1024 points. This example illustrates that the maximum of the energy functional aligns with the extremum of the signal. Figure <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">13</a> c) illustrates the uncertainty principle in wavelet analysis for low frequencies. Figure <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">13</a> b) shows that we have successfully circumvented the uncertainty principle, enabling simultaneous determination of signal localization and frequency analysis.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig13"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 13.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/0dd576c2ee60/12021_2024_9698_Fig13_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO13" height="1394" width="635" alt="Fig. 13"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig13/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>a) The segment of an EEG recording in lead Fp2 exhibiting ocular artifact and the superimposed energy functional <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq133"><math id="d33e5941"><mrow><mi>W</mi><mi>K</mi><mi>r</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow></math></span> within a window of 1024 points; b) wavelet spectrum derived from wavelet analysis for localization, and c) wavelet spectrum according to the wavelet analysis</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par68">The maximum of the energy functional does not always precisely align with the extremum of the signal due to the smooth nature of the energy functional, which approximates the impulse. Additionally, the impulse may include a high-amplitude and high-frequency component. Figure <a href="#Fig14" class="usa-link">14</a> illustrates an example of an impulse where there is a 15-point difference between the extremum and maximum of the energy functional <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq132"><math id="d33e5921"><mrow><mi>W</mi><mi>K</mi><mi>r</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow></math></span>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig14"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 14.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/25da76d00f26/12021_2024_9698_Fig14_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO14" height="1359" width="596" alt="Fig. 14"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig14/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>a) A segment of an EEG recording in lead T4 featuring the impulse component and the superimposed energy functional <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq134"><math id="d33e5963"><mrow><mi>W</mi><mi>K</mi><mi>r</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow></math></span> within a window of 1024 points; b) wavelet spectrum derived from wavelet analysis for localization, and c) wavelet spectrum obtained from the wavelet analysis</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par69">A limitation of spectral analysis, shared by wavelet analysis, is its susceptibility to variations based on the signal’s starting point. In this example, we demonstrate the invariance of the wavelet spectrum, generated using the proposed wavelet analysis method for localization, with respect to shifts in the impulse component of the signal. In classical wavelet analysis, the wavelet spectrogram of a signal is built by the scalar product of the signal with the daughter wavelets within the corresponding scale windows, determining the amplitude of the frequency components. In this case, the amplitude value is constant during the corresponding window Fig. <a href="#Fig15" class="usa-link">15</a> a) and b). Thus, for low frequencies (larger windows), according to the Heisenberg uncertainty principle, it is impossible to determine the localization of the impulse. Figures <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">13</a> - <a href="#Fig15" class="usa-link">15</a> c) and d) show the spectral powers of the signals, represented as the sums of the squares of the amplitudes resulting from the signal decomposition using the first four Krawtchouk functions within the scale windows W (k = 10, 9, ..., 2) with a length of 2n points (n = 10, 9, ..., 2). For this example, the wavelet spectrum was recalculated in Hz using EEG data sampled at 500 Hz. It is notable that the wavelet spectra for signals with a shifted impulse component exhibit significant differences, as illustrated in Fig. <a href="#Fig15" class="usa-link">15</a> c) and d).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig15"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 15.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11757888_12021_2024_9698_Fig15_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/b18484e35e61/12021_2024_9698_Fig15_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO15" height="534" width="765" alt="Fig. 15"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig15/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>a) A segment of an EEG recording in lead F3 featuring the impulse component; b)a segment of an EEG recording in lead F3 displaying the shifted impulse component and the superimposed energy functionals for the windows <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq135"><math id="d33e5999"><mrow><mi>W</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>W</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>9</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>W</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span> within the windows of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq136"><math id="d33e6022"><mrow><mn>1024</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>512</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></math></span> points and in accordance of the frequencies 0,5 - 125 Hz; c) wavelet spectrum obtained from wavelet analysis for the signal a); d) wavelet spectrum according to the wavelet analysis for the signal b)</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par70">In the context of wavelet analysis for the localization of the impulse component, we build wavelet spectrograms based on the energy functional. This functionals are defined as the sum of the squares of the convolution of the signal with the first four Krawtchouk functions, each shifted by the parameter <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq137"><math id="d33e6042"><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mspace width="4pt"></mspace><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>9</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> Fig. <a href="#Fig16" class="usa-link">16</a>). As a result, the maximum of the energy functional coincides with the localization of the extremum signal peak. Figures <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">13</a>, <a href="#Fig14" class="usa-link">14</a>, <a href="#Fig16" class="usa-link">16</a> show the spectral powers of the signals in terms of the energy functionals within the scale windows WKr (k = 10, 9, ..., 2) with a length of 2n points (n = 10, 9, ..., 2). Figure <a href="#Fig16" class="usa-link">16</a> demonstrates that, as a result of wavelet analysis for localization, we obtain spectral features of the impulse component of the EEG signal that are invariant to the shift of this impulse.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig16"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 16.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11757888_12021_2024_9698_Fig16_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3806/11757888/e0f16558fb0a/12021_2024_9698_Fig16_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO16" height="554" width="771" alt="Fig. 16"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig16/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>a) A segment of an EEG recording in lead F3 featuring the impulse component; b) a segment of an EEG recording in lead F3 displaying the shifted impulse component and the superimposed energy functionals for the windows <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq138"><math id="d33e6106"><mrow><mi>W</mi><mi>K</mi><mi>r</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>W</mi><mi>K</mi><mi>r</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>9</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>W</mi><mi>K</mi><mi>r</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span> within the windows of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq139"><math id="d33e6135"><mrow><mn>1024</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>512</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></math></span> points and in accordance of the frequencies 0,5 - 125 Hz; c) wavelet spectrum derived from wavelet analysis for localization for the signal a); d) wavelet spectrum derived from wavelet analysis for localization for the signal b)</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par71">The examples of numerical experiments fully confirm our hypothesis that the wavelet analysis method for localization allows us to simultaneously calculate both the time coordinates of an impulse and its frequency features.</p></section><section id="Sec12"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Conclusion</h2>
<p id="Par72">Our findings highlight the successful development of a method that simultaneously determines both the localization coordinates and frequency characteristics of a signal’s impulse component. This method integrates a model of invariant pattern recognition, inspired by the visual system, into our wavelet algorithm. For the first time, saccades are explicitly incorporated as a fundamental element of the visual system, providing the critical property of recognition invariance. By integrating the recognition invariance model with wavelet analysis, we developed an effective approach for analyzing non-stationary EEG signals with impulse components. This method was successfully applied to EEG experiments, demonstrating its capability for accurately determining both the precise localization and frequency characteristics of these impulse components. Furthermore, the generalized spectral features extracted from the impulse components can be utilized in machine learning algorithms, enabling efficient recognition and subsequent classification of EEG signals.</p>
<p id="Par73">As we have demonstrated in the examples N3, N4, N5, our method is most successful in tasks where it is necessary to detect the impulse component of the signal or to detect the repetition of reactions that do not have a rigidly defined rhythm (example N3). The method allows us to decompose signals into components at different scales (N4), and to restore the initial signal as the sum of these components with any accuracy specified in advance. At the same time, the method underperforms for analyzing signals with insufficiently expressed changes in amplitude.</p>
<p id="Par74">We plan to focus future research on developing a method for automatically removing artifacts from EEG signals, including single signals. In addition, based on this method, we plan to create a method for studying the dynamics of human brain connectivity using EEG recordings. We believe that this will enable researchers and medical experts to visualize the dynamics of existing neural networks and obtain information for analyzing multiple brain states and diagnosing neural network lesions. This method can be especially helpful in diagnosing invisible brain injuries on MRI and fMRI.</p>
<p id="Par75">The most challenging aspect of our proposed method is determining the system of thresholds across different scales in wavelet analysis. Currently, these thresholds are established through extensive numerical experiments on signals of a specific type. In the future, we aim to develop specialized methods for training the algorithms using a designated training sequence.</p>
<p id="Par76">We believe our contribution lays the groundwork for analyzing EEG signal dynamics, enabling the identification and classification of their impulse components. The capabilities of our method allow researchers to investigate non-stationary and transient processes in brain activity, as well as detect artifacts, anomalies, etc.</p></section><section id="Sec13"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Information Sharing Statement</h2>
